\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{j}}=&\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{j}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}}{\pi}\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}(y_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})-\varepsilon^{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}(x_{\varepsilon}-P)\cdot(y_{\varepsilon}-P)+\varepsilon^{4}}\\
&+\frac{\Lambda^{2}_{2}}{\pi}\frac{(y_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{|y_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{(\Lambda_{2}^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2})(y_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{\pi|y_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}+\big|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}\big|\right)\\
=&\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{j}}+O\left(\Big|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}\Big|\right)+o\big(1\big).\end{split}

(5.2)

To prove (
5.1
), we need to estimate the term
\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}
. First, we show that

\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}\to 0.

(5.3)

Indeed, suppose that
\frac{\varepsilon}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}\to A\in(0,1]

by contradiction. From
(
3.11
), we know that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=&\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{(B(P,\varepsilon))^{c}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{(\Lambda_{1}^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2})(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}}\\
&-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}+O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(5.4)

Since
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=0
and
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=O(1)
, then (
5.4
) gives

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{(B(P,\varepsilon))^{c}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}+\frac{(\Lambda_{1}^{2}+\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2})(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+1\right).\end{split}

(5.5)

On the other hand, by (
3.2
), we can compute

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{(B(P,\varepsilon))^{c}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=&-\frac{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}{\pi}\frac{(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}}{\pi}\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})-\varepsilon^{2}(y_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}(x_{\varepsilon}-P)\cdot(y_{\varepsilon}-P)+\varepsilon^{4}}\\
=&-\frac{\Lambda_{1}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})}{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{\big|\frac{1}{A^{2}}-1+o(1)\big|}+\frac{\Lambda_{2}+o(1)}{\frac{1}{A^{2}}+o(1)}\right)+O\big(1\big).\end{split}

(5.6)

So from (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), we get

\frac{x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{\big|\frac{1}{A^{2}}-1+o(1)\big|}=O\big(1\big)+o\Big(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}\Big),

which is not possible, and this proves (
5.3
).

Now putting (
5.3
) into (
5.4
) and using (
3.2
), we have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=&\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}+O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{3}}\right).\end{split}

(5.7)

Then using (
5.7
) and
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=0
and
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial x_{j}}=O(1)
, we have

\begin{split}\frac{(x_{\varepsilon,j}-P_{j})\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{3}}\right)+O\Big(1\Big),\end{split}

which, together with (
5.3
), gives

\begin{split}\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}=o\big(1\big).\end{split}

(5.8)

Now
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{j}}=0
,
(
5.2
) and (
5.8
) imply
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{j}}=o\big(1\big)
, and hence (
5.1
) follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

The case
x_{\varepsilon}\to P
and
y_{\varepsilon}\to y_{0}\neq P
is proved in Proposition
5.1
. The other case follows switching the role of
x
and
y
.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

Assume
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}
is a type II critical point of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
such that
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})\to(P,y_{0})
. If
the matrix

\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\ \text{ is invertible},

(5.9)

we have, for
\varepsilon\to 0
,

y_{\varepsilon}-y_{0}=-\left(\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{l}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq l,j\leq 2}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{l}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq l,j\leq 2}\Big(x_{\varepsilon}-P\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big).

(5.10)

Moreover, if
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\neq 0
, then, for
\varepsilon\to 0
,

x_{\varepsilon}=\frac{s_{\varepsilon}(1+o(1))}{\left|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\right|}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})+P,

(5.11)

where
s_{\varepsilon}\in\Big(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|},\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}}\Big)
is the unique solution of equation

h_{\varepsilon}(r):=\frac{\ln r}{r}-\frac{\pi}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}\Big|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{0}\big)\Big|\ln\varepsilon=0.

(5.12)

If, instead,
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})=0
, and (
5.9
) holds, we have, for
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\frac{x_{\varepsilon}-P}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}\to\eta_{0}~~~~\mbox{and}~~~~|x_{\varepsilon}-P|=r_{\varepsilon}\big(1+o(1)\big),

(5.13)

where
\lambda_{0}
is a positive eigenvalue of the matrix
\textbf{M}_{0}
(defined in Theorem
1.10
),

\eta_{0}
is a corresponding unit eigenvector and
r_{\varepsilon}
is the unique positive solution to
\frac{\ln r}{r^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=\frac{\lambda_{0}\pi}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}
.

Proof.

Repeating the same computation as before we get

\begin{split}&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}&\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\\[11.38109pt]
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}&\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{\varepsilon}-P\\[11.38109pt]
y_{\varepsilon}-y_{0}\\
\end{array}\right)+\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\\[8.53581pt]
&\,\,\,\,\,=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}\Big(x_{\varepsilon}-P\Big)\\[11.38109pt]
0\\
\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}O\left(|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}+|y_{\varepsilon}-y_{0}|^{2}\right)+o\Big(\big|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\ln\varepsilon}\big|\Big)\\[11.38109pt]
O\Big(|y_{\varepsilon}-y_{0}|^{2}+|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}+\big|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}\big|\Big)\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

(5.14)

If (
5.9
) holds, from the second line of (
5.14
), we have

\begin{split}y_{\varepsilon}-y_{0}=&-\left(\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\Big(x_{\varepsilon}-P\Big)\big(1+o(1)\big)+O\left(\left|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}\right|\right).\end{split}

(5.15)

If
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\neq 0
, since
x_{\varepsilon}\to P
and
y_{\varepsilon}\to y_{0}
, from the first line of (
5.14
), we immediately get (
5.11
). Moreover we claim that the function
h_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.12
) has a unique zero
s_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed
since
\frac{dh_{\varepsilon}(r)}{dr}=\frac{1-\ln r}{r^{2}}
, then
\frac{dh_{\varepsilon}(r)}{dr}>0
for
r\in(0,e)
,
\frac{dh_{\varepsilon}(r)}{dr}<0
for
r\in(e,\infty)
and
\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}h_{\varepsilon}(r)>0
. Moreover, it holds

\begin{cases}h_{\varepsilon}\big(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\big)=|\ln\varepsilon|\left(-\ln|\ln\varepsilon|+\frac{\pi}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}\Big|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{0}\big)\Big|\right)<0,\\[11.38109pt]
h_{\varepsilon}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}}\big)=\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}\left(-\frac{\ln|\ln\varepsilon|}{2}+\frac{\pi}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}\Big|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{0}\big)\Big|\right)>0,\end{cases}

which concludes the claim. Inserting the rate of
|x_{\varepsilon}-P|
in (
5.11
) into (
5.15
) we get (
5.10
) for
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\neq 0
.

Next, we consider the case when
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})=0
.
Putting (
5.15
) into the first line of (
5.14
), we get

\textbf{M}_{0}(x_{\varepsilon}-P)\big(1+o(1)\big)=\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}-P)+o\left(\left|\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|\ln\varepsilon}\right|\right).

(5.16)

Dividing by
|x_{\varepsilon}-P|
we get that
\frac{x_{\varepsilon}-P}{|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}\to\pm\eta
which is a unit vector and (
5.16
) becomes
\textbf{M}_{0}\eta=\lambda\eta
where
\lambda=\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x_{\varepsilon}-P|}{\pi|x_{\varepsilon}-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}
. So
\lambda
is a nonnegative eigenvalue of
\textbf{M}_{0}
and
\eta
a corresponding unit eigenvector and the rate of
|x_{\varepsilon}-P|
is given by
r_{\varepsilon}
. This proves (
5.13
), together with (
5.15
) concludes the proof of (
5.10
).
∎

Now we focus on the existence of type II critical points such that
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})\neq 0
.
We recall that for convex domain
\Omega
,
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x,y)
has no critical points.
From the necessary condition (
5.1
), to have type II critical points, (
5.1
) must have solutions.
Now we discuss the validity of equation (
5.1
)
when
\Omega
is a ball firstly.

Proposition 5.3
.

Assume that
\Omega=B(0,r)
is a ball centered at
0
and radius
r
such that
P\in B(0,r)
.
Then denoting by
d=dist\big\{P,\partial B(0,r)\big\}
, we have that
there exists
d_{0}>0
such that if

•

d>d_{0}
, then there is no solution to (
5.1
).

•

d=d_{0}
, then there is one degenerate solution
y_{0}(P)
to (
5.1
).

•

d<d_{0}
, then there are two nondegenerate solutions
y_{1}(P)
and
y_{2}(P)
to (
5.1
) such that

\lim_{d\to 0}|y_{1}(P)-P|=0~~~\mbox{and}~~~\lim_{d\to 0}y_{2}(P)=0,

where by "nondegenerate" we mean that
\det\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}\partial y_{k}}\right)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\neq 0
. Moreover, it holds

index\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,r)}(P,\cdot),y_{1}(P)\big)=-1\,\,\hbox{ and }\,\,index\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,r)}(P,\cdot),y_{2}(P)\big)=1.

(5.17)

Proof.

In order to simplify the notations assume that
\Omega
is the unit ball
B(0,1)
. Then we have

\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(x,y)=-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}{2\pi}\ln(1-|x|^{2})-\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{2\pi}\ln(1-|y|^{2})+\frac{\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}}{\pi}\ln\frac{|x-y|}{\sqrt{|y|^{2}|x|^{2}-2x\cdot y+1}}.

(5.18)

Then (
5.1
) becomes

\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=0,~~~\mbox{for}~~~j=1,2,

where, up to a rotation, we can assume that
P=(s,0)
, with
s\in[0,1)
.
Observe that

\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{\pi}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{2}y_{j}}{1-|y|^{2}}+\Lambda_{1}\frac{y_{j}-P_{j}}{|y-P|^{2}}+\Lambda_{1}\frac{P_{j}-|P|^{2}y_{j}}{|y|^{2}|P|^{2}-2P\cdot y+1}\right)~~~\mbox{for}~~~j=1,2.

(5.19)

Let us recall
P=(P_{1},P_{2})
and consider first the case
P_{2}=0
. We need to solve

\frac{\Lambda_{2}y_{2}}{1-|y|^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}y_{2}}{|P-y|^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}|P|^{2}y_{2}}{|y|^{2}|P|^{2}-2P\cdot y+1}=0.

(5.20)

Obviously
y_{2}=0
is a solution. Note that
|y|^{2}=y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}
(we already have that
|P|^{2}=s^{2}
). We claim that

\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{1-y_{1}^{2}-y_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{s^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}-2sy_{1}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}s^{2}}{(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})s^{2}-2sy_{1}+1}>0.

(5.21)

In fact, since

(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}-1)(s^{2}-1)\geq 0
, we have

s^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\leq(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})s^{2}+1
and

\frac{1}{s^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}-2sy_{1}}\geq\frac{1}{(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})s^{2}-2sy_{1}+1}\geq\frac{s^{2}}{(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})s^{2}-2sy_{1}+1}.

So we see that (
5.21
) holds and (
5.20
) has only the solution
y_{2}=0
.

Since
y_{2}=0
, we look for solutions to (
5.19
) with
y=(t,0)
for
t\in(-1,1)
. Then equation (
5.19
) becomes

\frac{\Lambda_{2}t}{1-t^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(t-s)}{|t-s|^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}s}{1-st}=0,~~~~\hbox{ for $(s,t)\in[0,1)\times(-1,1)$.}

(5.22)

Obviously, when
s=0
, (
5.22
) has no solutions. Then we consider the case
s>0
. And
in this setting the claim becomes:

•

If
s<\bar{s}
then there is no solution to (
5.1
).

•

If
s=\bar{s}
then there is one degenerate solution
t(s)
to (
5.1
).

•

If
s>\bar{s}
then there are two nondegenerate solutions
t_{1}(s)
and
t_{2}(s)
to (
5.1
) such that
t_{1}(s)\to 1
and
t_{2}(s)\to 0
as
s\to 1
.

Figure 4. The set of
h(s,t)=0

Now let us introduce the function (See Figure 4),

h(s,t)=\frac{\Lambda_{2}t}{1-t^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(t-s)}{|t-s|^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}s}{1-st}.

Our proof needs several steps.

Step 1: All solutions to
h(s,t)=0
verify
0<t<s
.

If
s\leq t<1
, we have that

h(s,t)>0
.
And then (
5.1
) does not have solutions.
Next assume that
t<s
. Recalling that
t>-1
, we get
1-ts>s-t
and then it holds

\frac{1}{s-t}-\frac{s}{1-ts}>0
.
This shows that

h(s,t)=\frac{\Lambda_{2}t}{1-t^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{s-t}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}s}{1-st}<\frac{\Lambda_{2}t}{1-t^{2}}.

And then
h(s,t)<0
if
t\leq 0
. This proves the claim of Step 1.

Step 2: There exists
\bar{s}\in(0,1)
such that
h(s,t)=0
has

\begin{cases}\hbox{at least $2$ solutions if }&s>\bar{s},\\
1\hbox{ solution if }&s=\bar{s},\\
\hbox{no solution if }&s<\bar{s}.\end{cases}

It is easy to see that for
s\to 0
and
t\in(0,s)
, we have
h(s,t)\to-\infty
and so
h(t,s)<0
for
t\in[0,s)
and
s
small.
Define

\bar{s}=\sup\big\{s\in(0,1]:h(s,t)<0,\;\forall\;t\in(0,s)\big\}>0.

Observe that since
h(1,t)=\frac{\Lambda_{2}t}{1-t^{2}}>0
we have that
\bar{s}<1
and then there is
\bar{t}\in(0,\bar{s}]
, such that
h(\bar{s},\bar{t})=0
. On the other hand,

\frac{\partial h(s,t)}{\partial s}=\Lambda_{1}\left(\frac{1}{(s-t)^{2}}+\frac{1+ts}{1-ts}\right)>0,\quad\forall\;t\in(0,s),

which gives
h(s,\bar{t})>0
if
s>\bar{s}
. Then by the intermediate value theorem for continuous functions the claim follows since
h(s,0)=\Lambda_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{s}+s\right)<0
, and
h(s,t)\to-\infty
if
t\to s-0
. Observe that if
s>\bar{s}
one zero lies
in
(0,\bar{t})
and the other is in
(\bar{t},s)
.

In next steps, we give additional properties of the zeros of
h(s,t)
.

Step 3: For
s=\bar{s}
, we have that
\bar{t}
is a singular zero for
h(\bar{s},t)
.

Since
\frac{\partial h(s,t)}{\partial s}>0
in the region
t<s
, by the implicit function theorem we get that the set of zeros of
h(s,t)
is a graph
s=\phi(t)
as in the Figure 4. So
\phi
verifies

h\big(\phi(t),t\big)=0.

Observe that
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(\bar{s},\bar{t})=0
, since by definition of
\bar{s}
, the function
h(\bar{s},t)
achieves its maximum at
t=\bar{t}
. This shows that
\bar{t}
is a singular zero for
h(\bar{s},t)
.

Step 4: For
s>\bar{s}
there exactly two non-singular zeros
t_{1}(s)
and
t_{2}(s)
. Moreover for
s\to 1
,
t_{1}(s)\to 1
and
t_{2}(s)\to 0
.

By the definition of
h(s,t)
we have that

\frac{\partial h(s,t)}{\partial t}=\frac{\Lambda_{2}(1+t^{2})}{(1-t^{2})^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{(s-t)^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}s^{2}}{(1-st)^{2}}.

(5.23)

Since

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left(\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\right)=2\Lambda_{1}\left(\frac{1}{(s-t)^{3}}+\frac{s}{(1-st)^{2}}+\frac{ts^{2}}{(1-st)^{3}}\right)>0,

and
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(\bar{s},\bar{t})=0
, by the implicit function theorem there exists a function
\psi(t)
, such that
\psi(\bar{t})=\bar{s}
, and

\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\big(\psi(t),t\big)=0.

(5.24)

Moreover, it holds

\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(s,t)<\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\big(\psi(t),t\big)=0\;\text{if}\;s<\psi(t),\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(s,t)>0\;\text{if}\;s>\psi(t).

(5.25)

The next claim will be crucial.

CLAIM: the curves
\psi=\psi(t)
and
\phi=\phi(t)
intersect only at
t=\bar{t}
where
\psi(\bar{t})=\phi(\bar{t})=\bar{s}
, and

\psi(t)>\phi(t)

if

t>\bar{t}
,
\psi(t)<\phi(t)

if

t<\bar{t}
.

Once we prove the above claim, we see from (
5.25
) that
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(s,t)\bigr|_{s=\phi(t)}<0
if
t>\bar{t}
, while

\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(s,t)\bigr|_{s=\phi(t)}>0
if
t<\bar{t}
. This
gives

\phi^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{\frac{\partial h(\phi(t),t)}{\partial t}}{\frac{\partial h(\phi(t),t)}{\partial s}}>0,

if
t>\bar{t}
, and
\phi^{\prime}(t)<0
if
t<\bar{t}
. Hence, for
s>\bar{s}
,

h(s,t)=0
has exactly two solutions.

Now we prove the claim.
Let us show that

\psi^{\prime}(t)>0.

By definition of
\psi
we have

\begin{split}\psi^{\prime}(t)=&-\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial t^{2}}(\psi(t),t)}{\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial s\partial t}(\psi(t),t)}=\frac{\underbrace{-\frac{\Lambda_{2}t\big(t^{2}+3\big)}{(1-t^{2})^{3}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{(\psi(t)-t)^{3}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}\psi^{3}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{3}}}_{=A(t)}}{\underbrace{\Lambda_{1}\left(\frac{1}{(\psi(t)-t)^{3}}+\frac{\psi(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{2}}+\frac{t\psi^{2}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{3}}\right)}_{>0}}.\end{split}

Let us show that
A(t)>0
. By (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) we get

\frac{\Lambda_{2}\big(1+t^{2}\big)}{(1-t^{2})^{2}(\psi(t)-t)}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}\psi^{2}(t)}{(1-\psi(t)t)^{2}(\psi(t)-t)}=\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{(\psi(t)-t)^{3}}.

(5.26)

Putting (
5.26
) into
A(t)
we have

\begin{split}A(t)=&\Lambda_{2}\underbrace{\left(-\frac{t\big(t^{2}+3\big)}{(1-t^{2})^{3}}+\frac{2\big(1+t^{2}\big)}{(1-t^{2})^{2}(\psi(t)-t)}\right)}_{=B(t)}+\Lambda_{1}\left(-\frac{\psi^{3}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{3}}+\frac{\psi^{2}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{2}(\psi(t)-t)}\right).\end{split}

It is easy to check that

\begin{split}-\frac{\psi^{3}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{3}}+\frac{\psi^{2}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{2}(\psi(t)-t)}=\frac{\psi^{2}(t)}{(1-t\psi(t))^{3}(\psi(t)-t)}\big(1-\psi^{2}(t)\big)>0.\end{split}

Next, we can compute that

\begin{split}B(t)(1-t^{2})^{3}(\psi(t)-t)=&(-t^{4}+3t^{2}+2)-(t^{3}+3t)\psi(t)\\
>&-t^{4}-t^{3}+3t^{2}-3t+2~~\big(\mbox{since $\psi(t)<1$}\big)\\
=&(1-t)(t^{3}+2t^{2}-t+2)\geq 0~~\big(\mbox{since}~~t\in(0,1)\big).\end{split}

Hence we get
B(t)>0
and then
\psi^{\prime}(t)>0
.

Now we are in position to show that the curves
\psi=\psi(t)
and
\phi=\phi(t)
intersect only at
t=\bar{t}
. Since
\phi(\bar{t})=\psi(\bar{t})
,
\phi^{\prime}(\bar{t})=0
and
\psi^{\prime}(\bar{t})>0
, we deduce
\psi(t)>\phi(t)

if
t-\bar{t}>0
is small. Let us assume that there exists
t_{1}>\bar{t}
such that
\phi(t_{1})=\psi(t_{1})
and
\psi(t)>\phi(t),~~t\in(\bar{t},t_{1}).

This gives

\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})\geq\psi^{\prime}(t_{1})>0.

On the other hand,
by (
5.24
),

\phi^{\prime}(t_{1})=-\frac{\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(\phi(t_{1}),t_{1})}{\frac{\partial h}{\partial s}(\phi(t_{1}),t_{1})}=-\frac{\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(\psi(t_{1}),t_{1})}{\frac{\partial h}{\partial s}(\phi(t_{1}),t_{1})}=0,

which is a contradiction. Hence, we have
\psi(t)>\phi(t)
if
t>\bar{t}
. Similarly, we can prove that
\psi(t)<\phi(t)
if
t<\bar{t}
.

We have proved that for each fixed
s>\bar{s}
,
h(s,t)=0
has exactly one solution
(s,t_{1}(s))
with
t_{1}(s)\in(\bar{t},1)
, and

h(s,t)=0
has exactly one solution
(s,t_{2}(s))
with
t_{2}(s)\in(0,\bar{t})
.
Moreover, they are both
non-singular,
since

\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\big(s,t_{2}(s)\big)<0,\quad\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\big(s,t_{1}(s)\big)>0.

(5.27)

Using (
5.19
) and (
5.21
), we find that

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}\big(P,t_{i}(|P|)\big)}{\partial y^{2}_{2}}>0.

It is easy to see that

\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}\big(P,t_{i}(|P|)\big)}{\partial y_{1}\partial y_{2}}=0.

Using these relations and (
5.27
), we conclude that

\nabla_{y}^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(P,y)
is non-singular at
t_{i}(|P|)
, and

index\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(P,t_{2}(|P|)\big)=-1\hbox{ and }index\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{B(0,1)}(P,t_{1}(|P|)\big)=1.

We end the proof by showing the behavior of
t_{1}(s),t_{2}(s)
as
s\to 1
. Recall that
t_{1}(s),t_{2}(s)<s
and by the definition of
h(s,t)
, we have

\frac{\Lambda_{2}t_{i}(s)}{1-t_{i}(s)^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{s-t_{i}(s)}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}s}{1-st_{i}(s)}=0,~~~\mbox{for}~~~i=1,2.

(5.28)

Up to subsequence we can assume that

t_{i}(s_{n})\to t_{i}\in[0,1],~~~\mbox{for}~~~i=1,2.

Then by (
5.28
) we get for
i=1,2
,

\Lambda_{2}t_{i}(s)\big(s-t_{i}(s)\big)\big(1-st_{i}(s)\big)-\Lambda_{1}\big(1-t_{i}(s)^{2}\big)\big(1-st_{i}(s)\big)+\Lambda_{1}s\big(1-t_{i}(s)^{2}\big)\big(s-t_{i}(s)\big)=0.

Passing to the limit as
s\to 1
we get

t_{i}(1-t_{i})=0,~~~\mbox{for}~~~i=1,2.

Since
t_{1}(s)>\bar{t}
we have
t_{1}(s)\to 1
and by
t_{2}(s)<\bar{t}
we get
t_{2}(s)\to 0
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

By Proposition
5.3
, there exists
d_{1}>0
such that for
d>d_{1}
the necessary condition (
1.9
) does not hold and this implies that there are no type II critical points that verify
x_{\varepsilon}\to P
and
y_{\varepsilon}\to y_{0}
. Switching the role of
x
and
y
in Proposition
5.3
we get the existence of
d_{2}>0
such that there are no type II critical points that verify
x_{\varepsilon}\to x_{0}
and
y_{\varepsilon}\to P
.
This proves
a)
.

To prove
b)
and
c)
we consider the case
d<d_{1}
and we will prove the existence of two critical points
(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})
, for
i=1,2
, such that
x_{i,\varepsilon}\to P
and
y_{i,\varepsilon}\to y_{i}(P)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
y_{i}(P)
for
i=1,2
are the unique solutions to
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=0
given by Proposition
5.3
. When
d<d_{2}
reasoning in the same way we can show the existence of other two critical points
(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})
, for
i=3,4
, such that
x_{i,\varepsilon}\to x_{i}(P)
and
y_{i,\varepsilon}\to P
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
x_{i}(P)
for
i=1,2
are the unique solutions to
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x,P)}{\partial x_{j}}=0
given by the analogous of Proposition
5.3
.

Let us define the vector field

\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\left(\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x-P|}{\pi|x-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}(x-P),\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)\right),

and the points
(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},y_{i}(P))
for
i=1,2
, where
\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}
is given by

\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}=\left(\frac{\pi s_{\varepsilon,i}^{2}\ln\varepsilon}{\Lambda^{2}_{1}\ln s_{\varepsilon,i}}\right)\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{i}(P)\big)+P,

where
s_{\varepsilon,i}\in\Big(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|},\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}}\Big)
is the unique solution of equation

h_{\varepsilon,i}(r):=\frac{\ln r}{r}-\Big|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{i}(P)\big)\Big|\ln\varepsilon=0.

For
i=1,2
we consider the set
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}:=B(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},\delta_{\varepsilon})\times B(y_{i}(P),\delta)
where
\delta_{\varepsilon}<<\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ln\varepsilon|}}
and

\delta
is so small that they satisfy

\overline{B(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\delta_{\varepsilon})\times B(y_{1}(P),\delta)}\cap\overline{B(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},\delta_{\varepsilon})\times B(y_{2}(P),\delta)}=\emptyset.

(5.29)

We want to show that, for
i=1,2
,

deg\Big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i},0\Big)=deg\Big(\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i},0\Big).

(5.30)

It is easy to see that the point
(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},y_{i}(P))
satisfies
\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},y_{i}(P))=0
and it is the unique zero of
\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
in
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}^{i}
by the choice of
\delta
and
\delta_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.29
). This implies that

\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\neq 0\ \hbox{ for }(x,y)\in\partial B_{\varepsilon}^{i}.

(5.31)

Recalling (
3.2
), for every
x\in B(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},\delta_{\varepsilon})
and for every
y\in B\big(y_{i}(P),\delta\big)
, we have
the following expansion, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{j}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial x_{j}}-\Lambda_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}+o(1)\right)\frac{\ln|x-P|}{\ln\varepsilon}\frac{(x_{j}-P_{j})}{|x-P|^{2}},\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}+o(1).\end{cases}

Hence
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
turns to be a small perturbation of
\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}
and so (
5.31
) implies, for
\varepsilon
small enough,

\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)\neq 0\ \hbox{ for }(x,y)\in\partial B_{\varepsilon}^{i},

and then we prove (
5.30
) by the homotopy invariance of the degree.

It lasts to prove that, for
i=1,2
,

deg\Big(\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i},0\Big)\neq 0,

(5.32)

which, by (
5.30
) proves the existence of at least one critical point
(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})
for
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}
. To do this we compute the Jacobian of the vector field
\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
at the points
(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},y_{i}(P))
,

J_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Big(A_{\varepsilon,j,k}(x)\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial x_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\\[11.38109pt]
0&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\\
\end{array}\right),

where

A_{\varepsilon,j,k}(x)=\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}{\pi}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(\frac{(x_{k}-P_{k})\ln|x-P|}{|x-P|^{2}}\right).

By Proposition
5.3
we know that the submatrix
\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}
is invertible in
y_{i}(P)
. Moreover

\det\Big(A_{\varepsilon,j,k}(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)})\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}=\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}|\big(1-\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}|\big)<0.

This shows that

sign\left(\det J_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)},y_{i}(P))\right)=-sign~\det\left(\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{i}(P))}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\right).

Then (
5.32
) holds and (
5.30
) gives

deg\Big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i},0\Big)\neq 0,

which shows the existence of at least one critical point
(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})
for
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}
. Let

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\Big\{(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon},|x-P|=O\big(s_{\varepsilon}\big),~~|y-y_{0}|=O\big(s_{\varepsilon}\big)\Big\},

where
s_{\varepsilon}
is the unique solution of (
5.12
)(See Proposition
5.2
), then from (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), we know that all the critical points of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
satisfying
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})\to(P,y_{0})
belong to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
.
Moreover for any
(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
, we have following estimate

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}{\pi}\left[\frac{\delta_{ij}}{|x-P|^{2}}-\frac{2(x_{i}-P_{i})(x_{j}-P_{j})}{|x-P|^{4}}\right]+\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}+o\left(1\right),\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}+o\left(\frac{1}{|x-P|}\right),\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}+o\left(1\right).\end{cases}

(5.33)

Hence by the definition of
\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
, we deduce that

\nabla^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\nabla\bar{L}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\Big(1+o\left(1\right)\Big),

which implies

\begin{split}&\det\big(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})\big)\\
&=\det\Big(A_{\varepsilon,j,k}(x_{i,\varepsilon})\Big)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\det\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{i,\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\Big(1+o\left(1\right)\Big)\\[2.84526pt]
&=-\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{4}}{\pi^{2}|x_{i,\varepsilon}|^{4}}\det\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{i,\varepsilon})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}\left(1+o\big(1\big)\right)\neq 0.\end{split}

This gives that when
d<d_{1}
there exist two type II critical points
(x_{1,\varepsilon},y_{1,\varepsilon})
and
(x_{2,\varepsilon},y_{2,\varepsilon})
that verify (
1.11
).
This proves that the critical point
(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})
for
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}
is nondegenerate and also unique in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}
. Since, all the type II critical points are contained in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i}
by Proposition
5.2
this gives also the exact multiplicity of the type II critical points. Then there exist exactly two type II critical points
(x_{1,\varepsilon},y_{1,\varepsilon})
and
(x_{2,\varepsilon},y_{2,\varepsilon})
that verify (
1.11
).

In the same manner when
d<d_{2}
two type II critical points

(x_{3,\varepsilon},y_{3,\varepsilon})
and
(x_{4,\varepsilon},y_{4,\varepsilon})
that verify (
1.12
) can be obtained. This proves
b)
and
c)
.
Finally (
1.13
) follows by (
5.17
) since

index\big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}},(x_{i,\varepsilon},y_{i,\varepsilon})\big)=index\big(\bar{L}_{\varepsilon},(\tilde{x}^{(i)}_{\varepsilon},y_{i}(P))\big)=-index\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,\cdot),y_{i}(P)\big).

∎

Remark 5.4
.

Since for
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}
,
\mathcal{KR}_{D}(x,y)=\mathcal{KR}_{D}(y,x)
for any domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
then when
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}
, we have that
d_{1}=d_{2}
.

In a general convex domain
\Omega
, it seems very difficult to get a complete result as in Proposition
5.3
. Some properties will be deduced in the next proposition.

Proposition 5.5
.

Assume
\Omega\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a bounded convex domain and

P\in\Omega
. Then, denoting by
d=dist\{P,\partial\Omega\}
we have that the equation

\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=0,~~\mbox{for}~~j=1,2

(5.34)

admits exactly two solutions
y_{1}(P)
,
y_{2}(P)
if
d=dist\{P,\partial\Omega\}
is small enough. Moreover, they are all nondegenerate.
Furthermore,
we have that

|y_{1}(P)-P|\to 0~~~\mbox{and}~~~y_{2}(P)\to Q~~~\mbox{as}~~~d\to 0,

where
Q
is the unique critical point of
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(x)
.
Finally we have that

index~\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,\cdot),y_{1}(P)\big)=-1~~~\mbox{and index}~~\big(\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,\cdot),y_{2}(P)\big)=1.

(5.35)

To prove Proposition
5.5
, we need an
asymptotic expansion of
G_{\Omega}(y,P)
and
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y)
as
d\to 0
and
|y-P|\to 0
.

G_{\Omega}(y,P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|y-P|}-H_{\Omega}(y,P)
, then it holds

\begin{cases}\Delta_{y}H_{\Omega}(y,P)=0,&\mbox{in}~~\Omega,\\
H_{\Omega}(y,P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|y-P|},&\mbox{on}~~\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Assume
P=(0,d)
, near
P
,
\partial\Omega
is given by
y_{2}=a_{1}y^{2}_{1}+O(|y_{1}|^{3})
with
a_{1}>0
since
\Omega
is convex.
We define
f(y)=\Lambda_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y)-2\Lambda_{1}G_{\Omega}(P,y)
. Let

\widetilde{f}_{d}(z):=f(dz)=\Lambda_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(dz)-2\Lambda_{1}G_{\Omega}(P,dz)~~~\mbox{with}~~~d:=dist\{P,\partial\Omega\},

where
z\in\Omega_{d}:=\big\{z:dz\in\Omega\big\}
.
Then we have following result.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any fixed large
R>0
, it holds

G_{\Omega}(P,dz)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}+\frac{\ln d}{2\pi}+o(1),~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R).

(5.36)

Proof.

Denote
u_{d}(z):=H_{\Omega}(P,dz)
. Then
u_{d}
is the solution of the following
problem

\begin{cases}\Delta u_{d}=0,~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}:=\big\{z,dz\in\Omega\big\},\\[5.69054pt]
u_{d}\big|_{\partial\Omega_{d}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|dz-P|}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\ln\frac{1}{|z-e_{2}|}-\ln d\Big],\end{cases}

where
e_{2}=(0,1)
. We also have

\partial\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R)=\big\{(z_{1},z_{2}),z_{2}=\phi(z_{1})=a_{1}dz_{1}^{2}+O(d^{2}|z_{1}|^{3}),~z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}<R^{2}\big\}.

Let
u_{1}
be the solution of

\begin{cases}\Delta u_{1}=0,~~~z_{2}>0,\\
u_{1}(z_{1},0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z-e_{2}|}.\end{cases}

Then
u_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z+e_{2}|}
.

Let
\varphi_{d}(z):=u_{d}(z)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln d-u_{1}(z)
, then
\Delta\varphi_{d}(z)=0
in
\Omega_{d}
. And as
d\to 0
,
\varphi_{d}\to\varphi

in
C^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})
. It is easy to see that
\varphi
is harmonic, and satisfies

\varphi(z_{1},0)=0.
This gives
\varphi=0
. Then (
5.36
) holds.

∎

Lemma 5.7
.

For any
x\in\Omega
with
|x-P|\leq Cd
for some constant
C>0
, it holds

\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(dx)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln(2x_{2})-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln d+o(1).

(5.37)

Proof.

Let

v_{d}(x,z):=H_{\Omega}(x,dz)
. Then

\begin{cases}\Delta_{z}v_{d}(x,z)=0,~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}:=\big\{z,dz\in\Omega\big\},\\[5.69054pt]
v_{d}(x,z)\big|_{z\in\partial\Omega_{d}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|dz-x|}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big[\ln\frac{1}{|z-\frac{x}{d}|}-\ln d\Big].\end{cases}

Let
\psi_{d}(x,z):=v_{d}(x,z)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln d-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z+\frac{(-x_{1},x_{2})}{d}|}
. Then
\psi_{d}(x,z)
is
harmonic in
\Omega_{d}
. By our assumption, we have
\frac{|x|}{d}\leq C
, and then

\begin{split}\psi_{d}(x,z)\big|_{z\in\partial\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R)}=&\frac{1}{4\pi}\ln\frac{|z+\frac{(-x_{1},x_{2})}{d}|^{2}}{|z-\frac{x}{d}|^{2}}\Big|_{z\in\partial\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R)}=o(1).\end{split}

That is

H_{\Omega}(x,dz)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z+\frac{(-x_{1},x_{2})}{d}|}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln d+o\big(1\big)
.
Then putting
z=d^{-1}x
, we have (
5.37
).
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Using the estimates for the harmonic functions, we can deduce the following estimates:

\begin{cases}\nabla_{z}\big[G_{\Omega}(P,dz)\big]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\nabla_{z}\big[\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}\big]+o(\frac{1}{d_{z}}),~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),\\[8.53581pt]
\nabla^{2}_{z}\big[G_{\Omega}(P,dz)\big]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\nabla_{z}^{2}\big[\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}\big]+o(\frac{1}{d_{z}^{2}}),~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),\end{cases}

and

\begin{cases}\nabla_{z}\big[\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(dz)\big]=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\nabla_{z}\left[\ln z_{2}\right]+o(\frac{1}{d_{z}^{2}}),~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),\\[8.53581pt]
\nabla^{2}_{z}\big[\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(dz)\big]=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\nabla_{z}^{2}\left[\ln z_{2}\right]+o(\frac{1}{d_{z}^{2}}),~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),\end{cases}

where
d_{z}:=dist\big\{z,\partial\Omega_{d}\big\}
,

Now we give the expansion of
\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)
.

Lemma 5.9
.

It holds

\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)=-\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{2\pi}\ln(2z_{2})-\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{\pi}\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}-\frac{(\Lambda_{2}+2\Lambda_{1})}{2\pi}\ln d+o(1),~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),

\nabla_{z}\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\nabla\Big(\Lambda_{2}\ln z_{2}+2\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}\Big)\right]+o\big(\frac{1}{d_{z}}\big),~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R),

\nabla^{2}_{z}\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\nabla^{2}\Big(\Lambda_{2}\ln z_{2}+2\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}\Big)\right]+o\big(\frac{1}{d_{z}^{2}}\big),~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d}\cap B(0,R).

Proof.

These estimates follow from Lemma
5.6
, Lemma
5.7
and Remark
5.8
.
∎

Let
F(z):=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\Big(\Lambda_{2}\ln z_{2}+2\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|z+e_{2}|}{|z-e_{2}|}\Big)
. Then we have following result.

Lemma 5.10
.

F(z)
has a unique critical point
z_{0}=(0,\alpha)
, with
\alpha=\frac{2\Lambda_{1}+\sqrt{4\Lambda^{2}_{1}+\Lambda_{2}^{2}}}{\Lambda_{2}}
. Furthermore,
z_{0}
is nondegenerate.

Proof.

First, we have

\frac{\partial F(z)}{\partial z_{1}}=\frac{4\Lambda_{1}z_{1}z_{2}}{\pi((z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+1)^{2}-4z_{2}^{2})}~~~\mbox{and}~~~\frac{\partial F(z)}{\partial z_{2}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{z_{2}}+4\Lambda_{1}\frac{z_{1}^{2}+1-z_{2}^{2}}{(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+1)^{2}-4z_{2}^{2}}\right].

Hence
F(z)
has a unique critical point
z_{0}=(0,\alpha)
, with
\alpha=\frac{2\Lambda_{1}+\sqrt{4\Lambda^{2}_{1}+\Lambda_{2}^{2}}}{\Lambda_{2}}
.

Furthermore,

\frac{\partial^{2}F(z)}{\partial z_{1}^{2}}\Big|_{z=(0,\alpha)}=\frac{4\Lambda_{1}z_{2}}{\pi((z_{2}^{2}+1)^{2}-4z_{2}^{2})}\Big|_{z_{2}=\alpha}\neq 0,\,\,\,~~~~~~\frac{\partial^{2}F(z)}{\partial z_{1}\partial z_{2}}\Big|_{z=(0,\alpha)}=0,

and

\frac{\partial^{2}F(z)}{\partial z_{2}^{2}}\Big|_{z=(0,\alpha)}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[-\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}+\frac{8\Lambda_{1}z_{2}}{(1-z_{2}^{2})^{2}}\right]\Big|_{z_{2}=\alpha}<0.

Thus
z_{0}
is the nondegenerate critical point of
F(z)
.

∎

Now, we prove the following result.

Lemma 5.11
.

The function
\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)

has a unique critical point
z_{d}=\big(o(1),\alpha+o(1)\big)

in
B(z_{0},\delta)
. Furthermore,
z_{d}
is nondegenerate.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.9
, we have

\begin{split}\nabla_{z}\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)=\nabla_{z}F(z)+o(1)~~~\mbox{and}~~~\nabla^{2}_{z}\widetilde{f}_{d}(z)=\nabla^{2}_{z}F(z)+o(1)~~\mbox{in}~~~B(z_{0},\delta).\end{split}

This gives that
\widetilde{f}_{d}
has a unique critical point in
B(z_{0},\delta)
, which is also nondegenerate.

∎

For any
y\in\Omega
, we denote
d_{y}=dist\{y,\partial\Omega\}
, then the following result holds.

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose that
y_{P}=(y_{1,P},y_{2,P})
is a critical point of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)
satisfying
|y_{P}-P|\to 0
as
d\to 0
. Then
as
d\to 0
,

d^{-1}y_{P}\to z_{0}.

In particular, the critical point
y_{P}
of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)
satisfying
|y_{P}-P|\to 0
as
d\to 0
is unique.

Proof.

After translation and rotation, we assume that
y_{P}=(0,d_{y_{P}})
, and

\partial\Omega\cap B(0,\delta)=\big\{(y_{1},y_{2}):y_{2}=a_{1}y_{1}^{2}+O(|y_{1}|^{3}),~y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}<\delta^{2}\big\}.

Let

w_{P}(z):=H_{\Omega}(P,d_{y_{P}}z)
. Then

\begin{cases}\Delta w_{P}=0,~~~\mbox{in}~~~\Omega_{d_{y_{P}}}:=\big\{z,d_{y_{P}}z\in\Omega\big\},\\[5.69054pt]
u\big|_{\partial\Omega_{d_{y_{P}}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|d_{y_{P}}z-P|}.\end{cases}

We claim that
\frac{|P|}{d_{y_{P}}}\to+\infty
is impossible.
Suppose that
\frac{|P|}{d_{y_{P}}}\to+\infty
. Then for any
R>0
,

\ln\frac{1}{|d_{y_{P}}z-P|}=\ln\frac{1}{|\frac{d_{y_{P}}}{|P|}z-\frac{P}{|P|}|}-\ln|P|=-\ln|P|+o(1),\hskip 9.24994ptz\in B(0,R).

This gives that

G_{\Omega}(P,d_{y_{P}}z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|d_{y_{P}}z-P|}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln|P|+o(1)=o(1),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{in}\;C^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}).

Hence from
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,d_{y_{P}}y_{P})=0
, we obtain

\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(d_{y_{P}}y_{P})=o(1).

This is a contradiction.

Now we assume that
d_{y_{P}}^{-1}P\to P_{1}
.
Then it holds

w_{P}(z)-\ln d_{y_{P}}\to w_{0}(z)~~\mbox{in}~~C^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}),

with

\begin{cases}\Delta w_{0}(z)=0~~\mbox{in}~~\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\[2.84526pt]
w_{0}(z_{1},0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z-P_{1}|}.\end{cases}

Hence
w_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{|z-\bar{P}_{1}|}
, where
\bar{P}_{1}

is the reflection point of
P_{1}
with respect to
z_{2}=0
. So we have

G_{\Omega}(P,d_{y_{P}}z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{|z-\bar{P}_{1}|}{|z-P_{1}|}+o(1).

From
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,d_{y_{P}}y_{P})=0
,
we find

\frac{P_{11}}{|P_{1}-(0,1)|^{2}}-\frac{P_{11}}{|P_{1}-(0,-1)|^{2}}=0,

(5.38)

and

\Lambda_{2}+2\Lambda_{1}\Bigl(\frac{1+P_{12}}{|(0,1)+P_{1}|^{2}}-\frac{1-P_{12}}{|(0,1)-P_{1}|^{2}}\Bigl)=0,

(5.39)

where we denote
P_{1}=(P_{11},P_{12})
.

From (
5.39
), we find that
P_{12}\neq 0
. Then (
5.38
)
gives that
P_{11}=0
, while (
5.39
) gives

P_{12}=\frac{-2\Lambda_{1}+\sqrt{4\Lambda^{2}_{1}+\Lambda_{2}^{2}}}{\Lambda_{2}}=\frac{1}{\alpha}=\frac{1}{d_{z_{0}}}.

Thus
z_{0}=\alpha P_{1}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

For
y\in B(Q,\delta)
, where
\delta>0
is small, let us consider the function

f(y)=\Lambda_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y)-2\Lambda_{1}G_{\Omega}(P,y).

We have that the critical points of
f
provide solutions to (
5.34
). Since
\Omega
is convex,
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}
admits exactly one
critical point
Q
, which is a nondegenerate minimum point.

We observe that for any
y\in B(Q,\delta)
,

G_{\Omega}(P,y)\to 0
in
C^{2}(B(Q,\delta))
as
d\to 0
. Hence
f(y)
is a
C^{2}
perturbation of
\Lambda_{2}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y)
for
y\in B(Q,\delta)
and
d
small. So
by the implicit function theorem,
f
has a unique critical point
y_{2}(P)
in
B(Q,\delta)
such that
y_{2}(P)
converges
Q

as
d\to 0
. Moreover,
y_{2}(P)
is a local minimum point of
f
and this
gives
\det(\nabla^{2}f(y_{2}(P))>0.

Next, Lemma
5.11
and Lemma
5.12
show that that

f(y)
has a unique critical point
y_{P}
, satisfying

|y_{P}-P|\to 0
as
dist\{P,\partial\Omega\}\to 0
.
This critical point is also nondegenerate.

Now we prove that the critical point
y_{P}
of
f(y)
satisfies
that as
d\to 0
,
either
|y_{P}-P|\to 0
, or
y_{P}\to Q
.
Indeed, suppose that
y_{P}\to\widetilde{y}
and
|P-y_{P}|\geq\delta>0
.
From
\Lambda_{2}\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}\big(y_{P}\big)=2\Lambda_{1}\nabla_{y}G_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{P}\big)
, while
\nabla_{y}G_{\Omega}\big(P,y_{1}(P)\big)\to 0
as
P
approaches the boundary (i.e.
d\to 0
). This implies that
\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(\widetilde{y})=0
and thus
y_{P}\to Q
.

In conclusion,
f(y)
has exactly two critical points, which are
nondegenerate. And finally, (
5.35
) holds by above discussions.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Since

\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=0
(
j=1,2
) has exactly two zero points,
which are nondegenerate, the proof of the existence part
is the same as that in Theorem
1.7
.

Considering the function
g(x)=\Lambda_{1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(x)-2\Lambda_{2}G_{\Omega}(x,P)
as in Proposition
5.5
, we get, when
d
is small, the existence of
x_{4}(P)\to Q
that satisfies (
1.10
). This gives the existence of the other critical point that verifies (
1.10
).

Now we turn to the proof of the non-existence part.
Let us show that
\nabla_{y}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})=0
is not verified, if
\Omega
is convex and
|P-Q|
is small, where
Q
is the unique critical point of
\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(x)
. We again use the argument in
[
20
]
.
We apply formula (
3.4
) in Lemma
3.1

with
a_{0}=a=P
and
b=y_{0}
and then

\int_{\partial\Omega}x\cdot\nu(x)\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,P)}{\partial\nu_{x}}\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,y_{0})}{\partial\nu_{x}}ds_{x}=-(y_{0}-P)\cdot\nabla_{y}G_{\Omega}(P,y_{0}).

(5.40)

Assume by contradiction that

\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{j}}=0
. This implies

\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{2\Lambda_{1}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y_{0})}{\partial y_{j}}\Rightarrow-(y_{0}-P)\cdot\nabla_{y}G_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})=-\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{2\Lambda_{1}}(y_{0}-P)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y_{0}).

(5.41)

In
[
10
]
it was proved that if
\Omega
is convex then the Robin function is strictly convex. In particular, its level set are strictly star-shaped with respect to
Q
. Hence for any
x\in\Omega
, it holds

\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(x)\cdot(x-Q)>0\Rightarrow-(y_{0}-P)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(y_{0})\leq C_{0}|P-Q|,

where
C_{0}>0
is independent of the point
Q
.
Using (
5.41
) we get that (
5.40
) becomes

\int_{\partial\Omega}x\cdot\nu(x)\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,P)}{\partial\nu_{x}}\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,y_{0})}{\partial\nu_{x}}ds_{x}\leq\frac{\Lambda_{2}C_{0}}{2\Lambda_{1}}|P-Q|.

(5.42)

On the other hand,
\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,P)}{\partial\nu_{x}}<0
,
\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,y_{0})}{\partial\nu_{x}}<0
. Also by the convexity of
\Omega
,
x\cdot\nu(x)\geq 0
, and then there exists
a nonzero measure set
A
such that
x\cdot\nu(x)>0
on
A
. Hence we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
, which is independent of the point
P
, such that

\int_{\partial\Omega}x\cdot\nu(x)\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,P)}{\partial\nu_{x}}\frac{\partial G_{\Omega}(x,y_{0})}{\partial\nu_{x}}ds_{x}\geq C_{1}.

(5.43)

So we have a contradiction by (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) when
|P-Q|
is small.
This ends the proof.
∎

Now we turn to the existence of type II critical points such that
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})=0
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

First, we observe that an asymptotic expansion of a type II critical point
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})
is proved in Proposition
5.2
, see (
5.10
) and (
5.13
).
Let
\eta^{(i)}
be a unit eigenvector of the matrix
\textbf{M}_{0}
related to the positive simple eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
.
Let us define, for
i=1,2
,

\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}=P\pm\eta^{(i)}r_{\varepsilon,i}\ ,\ \tilde{y}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}=y_{0}-\left(\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\Big(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P\Big),

where
r_{\varepsilon,i}
is the unique solution to
\frac{\ln r}{r^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=\frac{\lambda_{i}\pi}{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}

and the vector field

\begin{split}L_{\varepsilon}(x,y):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\\[11.38109pt]
\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial x_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x-P\\[11.38109pt]
y-y_{0}\\
\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x-P|}{\pi|x-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}(x-P)\\[11.38109pt]
0\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

Now using the homotopy invariance of the degree, it can be proved that

deg\Big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm},0\Big)=deg\Big(L_{\varepsilon}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm},0\Big),

(5.44)

where
B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm}
is as in (
5.29
) with
(\delta_{\varepsilon}^{i})^{3}<<\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}
. Using that for every
(x,y)\in B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm}
, it holds

\begin{split}\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\\[11.38109pt]
\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial x_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\Big)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x-P\\[11.38109pt]
y-y_{0}\\
\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x-P|}{\pi|x-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}(x-P)\\[11.38109pt]
0\\
\end{array}\right)\\[2.84526pt]
&+O\left(\delta^{2}+\left|\frac{\ln|x-P|}{|x-P|\ln\varepsilon}\right|\right).\end{split}

Finally, let us compute
deg\Big(L_{\varepsilon}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm},0\Big)
. Observing that

\begin{split}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}\ln|x-P|}{\pi|x-P|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}(x_{k}-P_{k})\right)\right|_{x=\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}}=&\frac{\Lambda_{1}^{2}}{\pi\ln\varepsilon}\left(\delta_{jk}\frac{\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}{|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|^{2}}+\eta_{k}^{(i)}\eta_{j}^{(i)}\frac{1-2\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}{|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|^{2}}\right)\\
=&\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}\left(\delta_{jk}\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|+\eta_{k}^{(i)}\eta_{j}^{(i)}\big(1-2\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|\big)\right),\end{split}

we have

\begin{split}&Jac\big(L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},\tilde{y}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})\big)\\
=&\begin{pmatrix}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial x_{j}}-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}\Big(\delta_{jk}\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|+\eta_{k}^{(i)}\eta_{j}^{(i)}\big(1-2\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|\big)\Big)\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\\[11.38109pt]
\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial x_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}&\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

And we know

\begin{split}&\det\big(Jac(L_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},\tilde{y}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})\big)\\
=&\underbrace{\det\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}}_{\neq 0}\det\left(\textbf{M}_{0}-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}\left(\delta_{jk}\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|+\eta_{k}^{(i)}\eta_{j}^{(i)}\big(1-2\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|\big)\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\right)\\
=&\lambda_{i}\frac{2\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|-1}{\ln|\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm}-P|}\left(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{i}\right)\neq 0,~~~\mbox{with $l\in\{1,2\}$ and $l\neq i$},\end{split}

because
\lambda_{i}>0
and
\lambda_{l}\neq\lambda_{i}
by assumptions.
This shows that
deg\Big(L_{\varepsilon}(x,y),B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm},0\Big)\neq 0
and by (
5.44
) there exists at least one critical point
(x_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},y_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})
for
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
in
B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm}
.

Now we get in the same way the nondegeneracy of the critical points
(x_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},y_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})
in the balls
B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm}
. In fact,
letting

\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\Big\{(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon},|x-P|=O\big(r_{\varepsilon}\big),~~|y-y_{0}|=O\big(r_{\varepsilon}\big)\Big\},

where
r_{\varepsilon}=\max\{r_{\varepsilon,i}\}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
From (
5.10
) and (
5.13
), we know that all the critical points of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
satisfying
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})\to(P,y_{0})
belong to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
.
Hence by (
3.3
) and (
3.12
), for any
(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
, we have following estimate

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}=-\frac{\Lambda^{2}_{1}\ln|x-P|}{\pi\ln\varepsilon}\left[\frac{\delta_{ij}}{|x-P|^{2}}-\frac{2(x_{i}-P_{i})(x_{j}-P_{j})}{|x-P|^{4}}\right]+\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}+o(1),\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial y_{j}}+o(1),\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}+o(1).\end{cases}

Hence by the definition of
L_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
, we deduce that

\nabla^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=\nabla L_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\Big(1+o(1)\Big)
,
which implies

\det\big(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},y_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})\big)=2\lambda_{i}\left(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{i}\right)+o(1)\neq 0,

with
l\in\{1,2\}
and
l\neq i
for
\varepsilon
small enough, we get the nondegeneracy of the critical point
(x_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},y_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})
of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
. This gives the uniqueness in the balls
B_{\varepsilon}^{i,\pm}
.

Moreover we have that

\begin{split}index\big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}},(x_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},y_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})\big)=&index\big(L_{\varepsilon},(\tilde{x}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm},\tilde{y}_{\varepsilon}^{(i),\pm})\big)\\
=&sign\left[\det\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(P,y_{0})}{\partial y_{k}\partial y_{j}}\right)_{1\leq k,j\leq 2}\left(\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{i}\right)\right],\end{split}

with
l\in\{1,2\}
and
l\neq i
.
Hence we have the existence of exactly four critical points, which are nondegenerate.
∎

6.
The existence of critical points of Type III

We now discuss critical points of Type III. For the simplicity of the notations, we assume that
P=0
. From now on, we assume that
both
x
and
y
are close to 0.

6.1.
The location of critical points

In this subsection, we prove Theorem
1.13
(1). Using that
P=0
and
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega}(x,y)}{\partial x_{j}}+2\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}\frac{\partial S(x,y)}{\partial x_{j}}=O(1)
, we rewrite (
3.11
) as

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial x_{j}}=\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{\pi}\left[-\frac{\Lambda_{1}x_{j}}{|x|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{2}(|y|^{2}x_{j}-\varepsilon^{2}y_{j})}{|x|^{2}|y|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x\cdot y+\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{2}(x_{j}-y_{j})}{|x-y|^{2}}-\frac{x_{j}}{|x|^{2}}\frac{\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right]+O\left(\frac{1}{|x|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+1\right),\\[8.53581pt]
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\Lambda_{2}}{\pi}\left[-\frac{\Lambda_{2}y_{j}}{|y|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}(|x|^{2}y_{j}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{j})}{|x|^{2}|y|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x\cdot y+\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(y_{j}-x_{j})}{|x-y|^{2}}-\frac{y_{j}}{|y|^{2}}\frac{\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y|}{\varepsilon}}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right]+O\left(\frac{1}{|y|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+1\right).\end{cases}

(6.1)

Proof of Theorem
1.13
(1).

We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1.
It holds

\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\to\infty~~~~~~~\mbox{and}~~~~~~~\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{|y_{\varepsilon}|}\leq C,~~\mbox{for some positive constant}~~C.

First, from
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})=0
and (
6.1
), we have

\begin{cases}\frac{\Lambda_{1}x_{\varepsilon,j}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{2}(|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}x_{\varepsilon,j}-\varepsilon^{2}y_{\varepsilon,j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}-\frac{\Lambda_{2}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-y_{\varepsilon,j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+\frac{x_{\varepsilon,j}(\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|x_{\varepsilon}|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+1\right),\\[8.53581pt]
\frac{\Lambda_{2}y_{\varepsilon,j}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}y_{\varepsilon,j}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon,j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(x_{\varepsilon,j}-y_{\varepsilon,j})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+\frac{y_{\varepsilon,j}(\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon})}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|y_{\varepsilon}|\cdot|\ln\varepsilon|}+1\right).\end{cases}

(6.2)

Then
\sum^{2}_{j=1}\big(x_{\varepsilon,j}\times\mbox{the first identity of \eqref{sec6-02}}\big)
, we get

\frac{\Lambda_{1}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{2}(|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}-\frac{\Lambda_{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot(x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}}{\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}+|x_{\varepsilon}|\right).

(6.3)

Also,

\sum^{2}_{j=1}\big(y_{\varepsilon,j}\times\mbox{the second identity of \eqref{sec6-02}}\big)
gives us

\frac{\Lambda_{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}(x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon})\cdot y_{\varepsilon}}{|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda_{1}\ln\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda_{2}\ln\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}}{\ln\varepsilon}=O\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}+|y_{\varepsilon}|\right).

(6.4)

Hence, letting
\tau:=\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{\Lambda_{2}}
, from
\frac{1}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\big(\Lambda_{1}\times\eqref{sec6-03}+\Lambda_{2}\times\eqref{sec6-04}\big)
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{\tau^{2}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{2\tau(|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}-\tau+\frac{(\tau+1)(\tau\ln\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}+\ln\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon})}{\ln\varepsilon}\\
&=O\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}+|x_{\varepsilon}|+|y_{\varepsilon}|\right),\end{split}

(6.5)

which gives
\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}\leq C
. Then there exists a constant
\delta>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\geq 1+\delta~~~~\mbox{and}~~~~\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\geq 1+\delta.

(6.6)

Now we claim

\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\to\infty~~~~~~~\mbox{and}~~~~~~~\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\to\infty.

(6.7)

We first prove that
\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\leq M,\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\leq M

does not occur. Suppose that
\frac{x_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\to w_{0}
and
\frac{y_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\to z_{0}
. In view of (
6.6
),
we see that
|w_{0}|,\;|z_{0}|>1
. Now
(
6.2
) gives
w_{0}\neq z_{0}
and

\begin{cases}\frac{\tau w_{0,j}}{|w_{0}|^{2}-1}-\frac{w_{0,j}-z_{0,j}}{|z_{0}-w_{0}|^{2}}+\frac{|z_{0}|^{2}w_{0,j}-z_{0,j}}{|z_{0}|^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}-2w_{0}\cdot z_{0}+1}=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\frac{z_{0,j}}{|z_{0}|^{2}-1}-\frac{\tau(z_{0,j}-w_{0,j})}{|z_{0}-w_{0}|^{2}}+\frac{\tau(|w_{0}|^{2}z_{0,j}-w_{0,j})}{|z_{0}|^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}-2w_{0}\cdot z_{0}+1}=0.\end{cases}

(6.8)

Let us show that system (
6.8
) has no solutions
and hence we obtain a contradiction. In fact, up to a suitable rotation we can assume that
w_{0,2}=0
. This also implies that
z_{0,2}=0
. Then there exists
\lambda\neq 1
such that
z_{0,1}=\lambda w_{0,1}
and

\begin{cases}\frac{\tau|w_{0}|^{2}}{|w_{0}|^{2}-1}+\frac{\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{4}-\lambda|w_{0}|^{2}}{|\lambda|w_{0}|^{2}-1|^{2}}=\frac{1-\lambda}{|\lambda-1|^{2}},\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}-1}+\frac{\tau(\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{4}-\lambda|w_{0}|^{2})}{|\lambda|w_{0}|^{2}-1|^{2}}=\frac{\tau(\lambda^{2}-\lambda)}{|\lambda-1|^{2}},\end{cases}

which gives us that

0<\frac{\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}-1}+\frac{\tau^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}}{|w_{0}|^{2}-1}=\frac{\tau(1-\lambda^{2}|w_{0}|^{4})}{|\lambda|w_{0}|^{2}-1|^{2}}=\frac{\tau(1-|w_{0}|^{2}\cdot|z_{0}|^{2})}{|\lambda|w_{0}|^{2}-1|^{2}}<0.

Here we use that
|w_{0}|>1
and
|z_{0}|>1
, this gives a contradiction.

Suppose that
\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\leq M
and
\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\to\infty
and assume that
\frac{x_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\to w_{0}
. Using

\begin{split}\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}\to 1,\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}\to 1,\end{split}

we derive
from (
6.5
) that

\frac{\tau^{2}|w_{0}|^{2}}{|w_{0}|^{2}-1}+\underbrace{\frac{(\tau+1)\ln|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\ln\varepsilon}}_{>0}=o\big(1\big),

which gives a contradiction. Similarly, we can
prove that
\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\to\infty
and
\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\varepsilon}\leq M

do not occur.

Now we prove

\frac{1}{C}\leq\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{|y_{\varepsilon}|}\leq C,~~\mbox{for some positive constant}~~C.

From (
6.7
), we find

\begin{split}\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}\to 1,\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}}\to 1,\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}}{|x_{\varepsilon}|^{2}|y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-2\varepsilon^{2}x_{\varepsilon}\cdot y_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{4}}\to 1.\end{split}

Let
\frac{|x_{\varepsilon}|}{|y_{\varepsilon}|}\to a_{0}
, with
a_{0}\in[0,\infty]
. If
a_{0}=0
, then

\frac{x_{\varepsilon}\cdot(x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon})}{|x_{\varepsilon}-y_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\to 0.

Thus
(
6.3
) gives

\frac{\tau\ln|x_{\varepsilon}|+\ln|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\ln\varepsilon}=o(1)
. While, by (
6.4
), it holds
\frac{\tau\ln|x_{\varepsilon}|+\ln|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\ln\varepsilon}=-\tau+o(1)
.
Hence a contradiction arises.
Similarly,
a_{0}=\infty

is impossible and this gives that
\frac{|w_{\varepsilon}|}{|z_{\varepsilon}|}\to a_{0}\in(0,\infty)
.

Step 2.
It holds

|x_{\varepsilon}|,|y_{\varepsilon}|\sim\varepsilon^{\beta},\mbox{~with $\beta=\frac{\tau}{(\tau+1)^{2}}$}.

First, by (
6.5
) and (
6.7
), we have

\frac{\tau\ln|x_{\varepsilon}|+\ln|y_{\varepsilon}|}{\ln\varepsilon}=\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}+o\big(1\big)
. Also
using that
\frac{1}{C}|y_{\varepsilon}|\leq|x_{\varepsilon}|\leq C|y_{\varepsilon}|
, we have

\frac{\ln|x_{\varepsilon}|}{\ln\varepsilon}=\beta+o\big(1\big)
, which
implies
|x_{\varepsilon}|\sim\varepsilon^{\beta}
and then
|y_{\varepsilon}|\sim\varepsilon^{\beta}
.

Step 3.

Let us compute the asymptotic of
x_{\varepsilon}
and
y_{\varepsilon}
.

Set
A=(A_{1},A_{2})
and
B=(B_{1},B_{2})
where

A=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{x_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},\,\,\,\,\,B=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{y_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}},\mbox{~with $\beta=\frac{\tau}{(\tau+1)^{2}}$}.

We will use a refinement of (
6.1
), obtained by (
3.13
).
Due to some cancellations, it will be necessary to consider an expansions up to second order.
Letting
(x_{\varepsilon},y_{\varepsilon})=(\varepsilon^{\beta}w_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{\beta}z_{\varepsilon})
and recalling (
3.2
), (
3.13
) becomes

\begin{cases}\frac{\pi\varepsilon^{\beta}}{\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}{\partial x_{j}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{\beta}z_{\varepsilon})=\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{|w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}-\frac{\tau\ln|w_{\varepsilon}|+\ln|z_{\varepsilon}|+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right)+\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}-z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}-w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right),\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{\pi\varepsilon^{\beta}}{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}{\partial y_{j}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{\beta}z_{\varepsilon})=\frac{z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}-\frac{\tau\ln|w_{\varepsilon}|+\ln|z_{\varepsilon}|+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right)-\frac{\tau(w_{\varepsilon,j}-z_{\varepsilon,j})}{|z_{\varepsilon}-w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right).\end{cases}

(6.9)

Passing to the limit we have that
A
and
B
satisfy

\begin{cases}-\frac{\tau A_{j}}{(\tau+1)|A|^{2}}+\frac{A_{j}-B_{j}}{|A-B|^{2}}=0,\\[11.38109pt]
-\frac{B_{j}}{(\tau+1)|B|^{2}}-\frac{A_{j}-B_{j}}{|A-B|^{2}}=0.\end{cases}

This implies that
A\neq B
and if
A_{j}=0
, then
B_{j}=0
.
Thus, we can assume that
|z_{\varepsilon,j}-w_{\varepsilon,j}|\geq C>0
for some
j
.
This also gives
|w_{\varepsilon,j}|\geq C^{\prime}>0
.

Next, from
\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}{\partial x_{j}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{\beta}z_{\varepsilon})=\frac{\partial\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}{\partial y_{j}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{\beta}z_{\varepsilon})=0
, we deduce from
(
6.9
) that

\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{|w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}=-\frac{1}{\tau}\frac{z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(\frac{\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}-z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}-w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)}{\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}-z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}-w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)}\right)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\frac{z_{\varepsilon,j}}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\Big(1+O\big(\varepsilon^{\beta}\big)\Big),

which implies

|z_{\varepsilon}|=\frac{|w_{\varepsilon}|}{\tau}\Big(1+O\big(\varepsilon^{\beta}\big)\Big)\hbox{ and }z_{\varepsilon,j}=-\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{\tau}\Big(1+O\big(\varepsilon^{\beta}\big)\Big).

(6.10)

Inserting (
6.10
) in the first equation of (
6.9
), we obtain

\begin{split}0=&\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{|w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}-\frac{(\tau+1)\ln|w_{\varepsilon}|-\ln\tau+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right)\\
&+\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{|w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)\right)+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)\\
\Rightarrow&0=\frac{w_{\varepsilon,j}}{|w_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\left(\frac{(\tau+1)\ln|w_{\varepsilon}|-\ln\tau+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}{\ln\varepsilon}+O\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\right)\right),\end{split}

which implies that

|w_{\varepsilon}|\to|A|=C_{\tau}
with
C_{\tau}=\tau^{\frac{1}{\tau+1}}e^{-\frac{2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)(\tau^{2}+\tau+1)}{(\tau+1)^{2}}}
. And in the same way, we get

|z_{\varepsilon}|\to|B|=\frac{C_{\tau}}{\tau}
.
This proves (
1.17
), concluding the proof of this part.
∎

6.2.
Existence and asymptotics

To prove existence of the critical points, we will start by (
6.1
) and look for the critical points of
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
that are close to those of the first term of the expansion.

Motivated by the necessary condition of the previous subsection, we set

x=\varepsilon^{\beta}w\hbox{ and }y=\varepsilon^{\beta}z\hskip 9.24994pt\hbox{with }\beta=\frac{\tau}{(\tau+1)^{2}}.

(6.11)

Now we analyze the limit function of
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
.
In view of (
6.9
), we consider the following system

\begin{cases}\frac{w_{j}}{|w|^{2}}\left(-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}-\frac{\tau\ln|w|+\ln|z|+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right)+\frac{w_{j}-z_{j}}{|z-w|^{2}}=0,\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{z_{j}}{|z|^{2}}\left(-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}-\frac{\tau\ln|w|+\ln|z|+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}{\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)}\right)+\frac{\tau(z_{j}-w_{j})}{|z-w|^{2}}=0,\end{cases}

(6.12)

whose solutions are given by

\big(w,-\frac{w}{\tau}\big)~~~\mbox{with}~~~|w|=C_{\tau}=\tau^{\frac{1}{\tau+1}}e^{-\frac{2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)(\tau^{2}+\tau+1)}{(\tau+1)^{2}}}.

(6.13)

Observe that the solutions
(w,z)
to (
6.12
) are the critical points of the following function

F_{\varepsilon}(w,z)=-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}\big(\tau\ln|w|+\ln|z|\big)+\tau\ln|w-z|-\frac{\big(\tau\ln|w|+\ln|z|+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)\big)^{2}}{2\big(\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)\big)}.

Next we set

\begin{split}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})=&F_{\varepsilon}\big((\tilde{w},0),(\tilde{z},0)\big)\\
=&-\frac{\tau}{\tau+1}\Big(\tau\ln\tilde{w}+\ln(-\tilde{z})\Big)+\tau\ln|\tilde{w}-\tilde{z}|-\frac{\big(\tau\ln\tilde{w}+\ln(-\tilde{z})+2\pi\frac{\tau^{2}+\tau+1}{\tau+1}\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0)\big)^{2}}{2(\ln\varepsilon+2\pi\mathcal{R}_{\Omega}(0))},\end{split}

for
\tilde{w},\tilde{z}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{w}>0
and
\tilde{z}<0
.
Then, critical points of
F_{\varepsilon}(w,z)
are given by

\left\{\left(T\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{w}_{0}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\,T\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{z}_{0}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right),\,\,T\in O(2)\right\},

where
(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})
is a critical point of
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
.
Moreover, by (
6.13
), it follows that
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
has a unique critical point in the set
\tilde{w}>0
and
\tilde{z}<0
, given by

\tilde{w}_{0}=C_{\tau}
and

\tilde{z}_{0}=-\frac{C_{\tau}}{\tau}
.

In the next proposition, we show that
(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})
is a minimum for
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
.

Proposition 6.1
.

The function
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
admits a unique critical point
(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})
. Moreover, it is a nondegenerate and minimum point.

Proof.

The uniqueness of the critical point follows directly from (
6.13
). By straightforward computations, we have that

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial\tilde{w}^{2}}(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})=\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tilde{w}_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{(\tau+1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{\ln\varepsilon}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right)>0,\\
&\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial\tilde{w}\partial\tilde{z}}(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})=\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tilde{w}_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{\tau}{(\tau+1)^{2}}+\frac{1}{\ln\varepsilon}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right),\\
&\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial\tilde{z}^{2}}(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})=\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tilde{w}_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\tau+1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{\ln\varepsilon}\right)+o\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right)>0.\end{split}

Hence for
\varepsilon
small enough,

\begin{split}&\det\nabla^{2}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})=-\frac{\tau^{4}}{\tilde{w}_{0}^{4}\ln\varepsilon}+o\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right)>0,\end{split}

which gives the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.13
(2).

Let
(\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0})
be the unique critical point of
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
in
\tilde{w}>0
and
\tilde{z}<0
.
By Proposition
6.1
, we know that
(\widetilde{w}_{0},\widetilde{z}_{0})
is a minimum point of
\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})
.
Let
\delta_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|^{2}}
. For
\varepsilon
small enough we have that
\overline{B\big((\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0}),\delta_{\varepsilon}\big)}\cap\Big(\{\tilde{w}=0\}\cup\{\tilde{z}=0\}\Big)=\emptyset
.
Next, we define

\begin{split}B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}=&\Big\{(w,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{4},\hbox{ such that }\exists\,~\mbox{a rotation}~\,T\in O(2),\,\,(Tw,Tz)=\big((\widetilde{w},0),(\widetilde{z},0)\big),\,\,(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})\in B\big((\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0}),\delta_{\varepsilon}\big)\Big\}.\end{split}

We have
the following alternative:

•

The function
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
has infinitely many critical points.

•

The critical points of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
are isolated.

In the first case we get the second case in the existence part of Theorem
1.13
(2).
Next we assume that the critical points of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
are isolated.
We want to show that, for
\beta
as in (
6.11
),

deg\big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z),{B}^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}},0\big)=0.

(6.14)

We start by showing that

\begin{split}\Big\langle\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z),\nu\Big\rangle>0,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\;(w,z)\in\partial B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}},\end{split}

(6.15)

where
\nu
is the outward unit normal of
\partial B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
at
(w,z)
.

Indeed for any
(w,z)\in\partial B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
, we have that

\Big\langle\nabla F_{\varepsilon}(w,z),\nu\Big\rangle=\Big\langle\nabla\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z}),\tilde{\nu}\Big\rangle,

(6.16)

where
(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})\in\partial B\big((\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0}),\delta_{\varepsilon}\big)
and
\widetilde{\nu}=\frac{(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})-(\widetilde{w}_{0},\widetilde{z}_{0})}{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
is the outward unit normal of
\partial B\big((\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{0}),\delta_{\varepsilon}\big)
at
(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})
.
Then it holds, using that the
\nabla^{3}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{w},\tilde{z})
is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
in a neighborhood of
(\widetilde{w}_{0},\widetilde{z}_{0})
,

\begin{split}\Big\langle\nabla\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z}),\widetilde{\nu}\Big\rangle=&\Big\langle\nabla\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{z})-\nabla\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{w}_{0},\widetilde{z}_{0}),\widetilde{\nu}\Big\rangle\\
=&\delta_{\varepsilon}\Big\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{w}_{0},\widetilde{z}_{0})\cdot\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\nu}\Big\rangle+O\big(\delta_{\varepsilon}^{2}\big)\geq\delta_{\varepsilon}\left[-\frac{c_{0}(\tau)}{\ln\varepsilon}+o\left(\frac{1}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right)\right]>0,\end{split}

where
c_{0}(\tau)
is a positive constant that depends only on
\tau
,
by the choice of
\delta_{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon
small enough. Hence by (
6.16
), it holds

\big\langle\nabla F_{\varepsilon}(w,z),\nu\big\rangle>0.

Moreover, by (
6.9
), we have that

\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)=\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}\pi}\Big[\nabla F_{\varepsilon}(w,z)+O(\varepsilon^{\beta})\Big],

which implies that, for every
(w,z)\in\partial B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
, for
\varepsilon
small enough,

\begin{split}\Big\langle\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z),\nu\Big\rangle=&\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}\pi}\left[\Big\langle\nabla F_{\varepsilon}(w,z),\nu\Big\rangle+O(\varepsilon^{\beta})\right]\geq\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}\pi}\left[-\frac{c_{0}(\tau)\delta_{\varepsilon}}{\ln\varepsilon}+o\left(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}}{|\ln\varepsilon|}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{\beta})\right]\\
=&\frac{\Lambda_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{\beta}\pi(\ln\varepsilon)^{3}}\big(-c_{0}(\tau)+o(1)\big)>0.\end{split}

By (
6.15
) and the Poincaré-Hopf Theorem, we have

\deg\big(\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z),B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}},0\big)=\chi(B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}})=\chi(\mathbb{S}^{1})=0,

where
\chi(S)
is the Euler characteristic of
S
.

Next since
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
is continuous in
\overline{B}^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
and
\langle\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z),\nu\rangle>0
for
(w,z)\in\partial B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
by (
6.15
),
then
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
has a minimum in
B^{*}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
. Since the minimum has index
1
and by (
6.14
),
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon^{\beta}w,\varepsilon^{\beta}z)
admits at least another critical point with negative index.

Note that the above arguments hold for any function which is a
C^{1}

perturbation of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
.
This concludes that
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
has
at least two stable critical points. Hence we finish the proof of Theorem
1.13
(2)
.
∎

7.
The exact multiplicity of type III critical points

As stated in Section
6
,
to prove the existence of type III critical points of
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
, (
6.9
) is sufficient.
However, we can only determine the length, not the
direction of the critical points from
(
6.9
), because in the expansion of (
6.9
), the effects from
the location of the small hole and the geometric properties of
\Omega

are totally ignored. To determine the
direction of the critical points,
further
expansion for
\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
is necessary, so that
the effects from the location and from the geometry of
\Omega
can be captured.

Our strategies in the section consist of the following steps.

•

We expand
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
until
the effects from the location of hole and from the geometry of
\Omega
can be captured, in the sense that
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
can be written as

\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)={\bf{K}}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)+h.o.t.,

(7.1)

and we can find the exact number of the solutions for
{\bf{K}}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=0

and prove their non-degeneracy.

•

Using (
7.1
), we prove the existence of solutions for

\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=0
by showing the degree of this
vector field is not zero in each
small neighborhood of the solutions for
{\bf{K}}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=0
.

•

We prove that each the solution of
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=0

is nondegenerate and compute the index of each solution.

•

We prove the local uniqueness of solution of
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=0
near every solution of

{\bf{K}}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=0
by comparing the local degree of each solution

\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)=0
with the total degree
of the vector field
\nabla\mathcal{KR}_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(x,y)
in each
small neighborhood of the solutions for
{\bf{K}}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=0
.
